List Decodable Quantum LDPC Codes
Thiago Bergamaschi, Fernando Granha Jeronimo, Tushant Mittal, Shashank Srivastava, Madhur Tulsiani

Abstract




largesymbols"0C

We give a construction of Quantum Low-Density Parity Check (QLDPC) codes with near-optimal rate-distance tradeoff and efficient list decoding up to the Johnson bound in polynomial time.
Previous constructions of list decodable good distance quantum codes either required access to a classical side channel or were based on algebraic constructions that preclude the LDPC property.

Our construction relies on new algorithmic results for codes obtained via the quantum analog of the distance amplification scheme of Alon, Edmonds, and Luby [FOCS 1995]. These results are based on convex relaxations obtained using the Sum-of-Squares hierarchy, which reduce the problem of list decoding the distance amplified codes to unique decoding the starting base codes. Choosing these base codes to be the recent breakthrough constructions of good QLDPC codes with efficient unique decoders, we get efficiently list decodable QLDPC codes.

1
Introduction

The area of quantum error correction has had tremendous progress in recent years, particularly in the construction of quantum low-density parity-check (QLDPC) codes. These are codes where membership can be tested via checks acting only on a small number of qubits, which is often an important property for physical implementations.
Starting with the classic toric code by Kitaev
[
Kit03
]
, a sequence of works
[
TZ14
,
EKZ20
,
KT21
,
HHO21
,
PK22
,
LZ22
]
has led to the construction of
asymptotically good
QLDPC codes, with constant rate and constant relative distance.

Specifically, the above constructions yield a special form of quantum code known as
Calderbank–Shor–Steane (CSS) code, which can be specified by a pair of subspaces
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}\subseteq{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
satisfying
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
(which also implies
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}
). In this work, we will always take quantum codes to mean quantum CSS codes and refer to them
as QLDPC codes when
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
have generating sets consisting of sparse vectors. The code
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
is said to have blocklength
n
and alphabet size
q
, with the relative distance
\delta
and the rate
\rho
defined as,

\delta~=~\frac{1}{n}\cdot\min\left\{\lvert c\rvert~\Big|~c\in(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp})\cup(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp})\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho~=~\frac{1}{n}\cdot\left\lparen\dim(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})-\dim(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp})\right\rparen\,.

The codes are said to be
good codes
if both
\delta
and
\rho
are constants independent of
n
.

The first construction of good QLDPC codes was obtained by the recent breakthrough construction of
Panteleev and Kalachev
[
PK22
]
, also closely related to the independent construction
of good locally testable codes by Dinur, Evra, Livne, Lubotzky, and Mozes
[
DEL+22
]
. Subsequent variants of their
constructions by Leverrier and Zémor
[
LZ22
,
LZ23
]
and by Dinur, Hsieh, Lin, and Vidick
[
DHLV23
]
have also obtained simpler descriptions and stronger properties, including
algorithmic
guarantees such as the existence of linear-time (and even parallelizable) unique-decoding algorithms, for decoding from errors up to a constant fraction of the blocklength.

Improved error correction via list decoding.

Given the existence of codes with constant relative distance and associated unique-decoding algorithms, it is natural to consider the
maximum
fraction of errors that can be efficiently corrected and whether even stronger forms of decoding are possible for such codes.

For a code with relative distance
\delta
, unique-decoding can recover from, at most, a
\delta/2
fraction of errors (although existing unique-decoding algorithms for quantum codes do not necessarily achieve this threshold).
For several classical codes, it is possible to correct significantly more than
\delta/2
fraction
of errors by relaxing the decoding task to
list decoding

[
Gur01
,
Gur07
]
, where the goal is not to output a single codeword but possibly a (small) list of codewords within a given error radius
\tau
of the received word.
In addition to allowing for recovery from
\tau>\delta/2
fraction of errors, this is also helpful for several applications in complexity theory
[
Tre04
]
where tolerating a larger error radius is significantly more important than recovering a unique codeword.

In the quantum case, no-cloning restrictions forbid duplication of quantum states and require the task of list decoding to be defined more carefully. The appropriate analog in the quantum case is actually the
classical
task of recovering a list of error patterns with at most
\tau
-fraction of errors that can lead to a given received word (or rather, a given syndrome, which corresponds to the output of the parity checks).
The question of constructing list decodable quantum codes (but not necessarily QLDPC codes) was considered by Leung and Smith
[
LS08
]
, and more recently, quantum codes admitting efficient list decoding algorithms were also constructed in the work of Bergamaschi, Golowich, and Gunn
[
BGG24
]
.

Another form of decoding for quantum codes, which can go beyond the unique-decoding radius
\delta/2
, is where one is still trying to recover a single quantum state but is allowed to make an exponentially small (in the block-length
n
of the code) error in the output quantum state. This notion of approximate quantum error-correcting codes (AQECCs) was previously considered by Crépau, Gottesman, and Smith
[
CGS05
]
, and has found renewed recent interest
[
HP20
,
Ber24
,
YYGL24
]
.
The work of Bergamaschi, Golowich, and Gunn was also motivated by the construction of AQECCs and shows that existing constructions of purity testing codes
[
BCG+02
]
and robust secret sharing schemes
[
CDD+15
]
can be combined with list decodable quantum codes, to obtain AQECCs (with the same decoding radius).
Thus, constructions of quantum codes with efficient list decoding can be used for both the above forms of error correction beyond the unique decoding radius.

In light of the above discussion, it is desirable to have quantum codes that are both, efficiently list decodable and LDPC. Constructing such list decodable LDPC codes is challenging, even for classical codes. This is because most
list decoding techniques rely upon algebraic structure, which does not necessarily allow for the LDPC property.

1.1
Our Results

We give the first construction of QLDPC codes that are efficiently list decodable beyond the unique decoding radius. We can decode upto the
Johnson bound
defined as
\mathcal{J}(\delta):=1-\sqrt{1-\delta}
. It is not known how to construct QLDPC codes, or indeed even classical LDPC codes, which can be efficiently list decoded beyond the Johnson bound.

1.1
Informal version of
Theorem

7.9
.

For any constant
0<\rho<1
, and small enough
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}>0
, there is an infinite family of quantum LDPC codes over a constant-sized alphabet,
q(\varepsilon_{1})
, such that:

(i)

Each code can be constructed deterministically in time polynomial in its blocklength,
n
.

(ii)

The rate of each code is at least
\rho
, and the relative distance is
\delta\geq\frac{1-\rho-\varepsilon_{1}}{2}
.

(iii)

Each code can be list decoded from radius
{\mathcal{J}}\big\lparen\frac{1-\rho-\varepsilon_{1}}{2}\big\rparen-\varepsilon_{2}
in time,
n^{{\mathcal{O}}_{\varepsilon_{1}}(1/\varepsilon_{2}^{4})}
.

Our construction relies on a quantum analog of the distance amplification procedure due to Alon, Edmonds, and
Luby
[
AEL95
]
. The Alon–Edmonds–Luby (AEL) procedure takes a balanced bipartite graph —

G=(L,R,E)
with
\lvert L\rvert=\lvert R\rvert=n
and degree
d
— and two quantum CSS codes: an “inner code”

\mathcal{C}
of blocklength
d
and an “outer code”
\mathcal{D}
of blocklength
n
.
It combines these via concatenation and folding to construct a new quantum code
\mathcal{F}
with improved distance properties. Additionally, if the outer code
\mathcal{D}
is QLDPC and the degree,
d
, is a constant, then the final code
\mathcal{F}
is also QLDPC.

We show that this procedure yields a general method for obtaining list decodable codes, using only
unique-decodability
of the starting outer code.

1.2
Informal version of
Theorem

7.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the CSS code obtained by applying quantum AEL amplification to the outer code
\mathcal{D}
and inner code
\mathcal{C}
using an expander graph such that
\mathcal{F}
has distance
\delta\approx\delta(\mathcal{C})
. If the outer code has an efficient unique-decoder, then
\mathcal{F}
can be efficiently list decoded up to error radius
\tau\approx\mathcal{J}(\delta)>\delta/2
.

We obtain
Theorem

1.1
by picking the outer codes to be the recent constructions of unique-decodable QLDPC codes
[
LZ23
]
. We choose the (constant-sized) inner code,
\mathcal{C}
, to be an optimal CSS code matching the Singleton bound, and get
\delta\approx\delta(\mathcal{C})\approx 1/2
and
\tau\approx\mathcal{J}(\delta)\approx 0.293>0.25
.

1.2
Technical Overview

Our results are based on the framework developed by
[
JST23
]
for list decoding of (classical) codes up to
the Johnson bound, using
“covering lemmas”
and
“proofs of distance”
that can be captured by convex relaxations in the Sum-of-Squares (SoS) hierarchy of semidefinite programs.
The covering lemma ensures that the solution to the SoS convex relaxation captures sufficient
information about each element of the list. Given the covering, the list elements are then isolated
using the fact that different codewords are sufficiently far from each other.
The SoS framework requires expressing the proof of distance for the code being decoded, in terms of inequalities obtained via non-negativity of sum-of-squares of low-degree polynomials (in formal variables corresponding to codeword symbols).

For the case of codes obtained via the AEL distance amplification procedure, their framework gives a reduction — using convex relaxations in the SoS hierarchy — from the task of list decoding the resulting codes to that of unique-decoding the outer code.
The reduction relies on the observation that the distance proofs for codes constructed via AEL can
be expressed as the sum of squares of low-degree polynomials.
This is because AEL distance amplification is carried out using constant-degree expander graphs, and distance proofs are based on spectral inequalities (which correspond to quadratic forms of positive semidefinite matrices arising from the underlying graph).
Our result can be viewed as a quantum analog of this reduction.

Quantum Extensions and challenges

Extending the above framework for quantum codes faces some important bottlenecks. There are two key aspects to this generalization: one is that the notion of distance itself is different for quantum codes, and the second is that we need a proof that is expressible as sum-of-squares of low-degree polynomials.

Quantum Distance

For a classical code
C\subseteq{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
, the distance between
u,v\in C
is defined as the Hamming distance
\Delta(u,v)
between these vectors, which is easily expressible as a low-degree polynomial in (a real-valued embedding of) the coordinates of
u
and
v
.
For a CSS quantum code
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
the distance between (say)
u,v\in\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
is defined as the distance between
cosets
of
\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
containing
u
and
v
i.e.
\min_{w\in{\mathcal{F}^{\perp}_{Z}}}\Delta(u,v-w)
.

Low-Degree Proof

A common technique in proving distance between
u,v\in\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
that belong to different cosets of
\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
is to replace
u
by
u^{*}
, where
u^{*}
is the closest element of
u+\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
to
v
. This optimality of
u^{*}
with respect to distance from
v
gives it extra structural properties, which are then exploited to prove distance
\Delta(u^{*},v)=0
or
\Delta(u^{*},v)>\delta
. When working with SoS, however, the trivial statement
“
\Delta(u,v)\geq\Delta(u^{*},v)
”
need not have a low-degree proof!

The change in distance notions actually presents a significant barrier to implementing the “proofs to algorithms" approach. In fact, an important SoS lower bound by Hopkins and Lin
[
HL22
]
shows that even the
definition
of quantum distance cannot be captured by SoS, and even exponential-sized semidefinite programs from the SoS hierarchy cannot distinguish codewords at distance zero (in the same coset) from those that are far apart! This is overcome in the present work by a
new proof
of distance amplification for quantum codes via the AEL procedure, which lets us separate “local" vs "global" aspects of the distance for the resulting codes.

Our main technical contribution is thus to give a low-degree proof of the distance bound for the quantum version of the AEL procedure. The other known proof of quantum AEL
[
BGG24
]
uses the unique decoding radius and avoids dealing with
\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
. While it is an easy argument that the unique-decoding radius is half of the distance, this fact is not necessarily captured as a statement in terms of low-degree polynomials.

In contrast, our proof uses the observation that the space
\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
, for codes obtained by AEL amplification, has additional structure and can be decomposed as
\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{out}}+\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{in}}
. The distance minimization is then done in two steps corresponding to the two spaces.

We define the notion of a
partial minimizer
by utilizing decomposition of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{in}}
into local subspaces,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{in}}={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
. For any fixed pair
u,v\in\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
, the
partial minimizer
maps
u
to
u^{\prime}\in u+\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{in}}
, instead of the optimal
u^{*}
which is closest to
v
. This
u^{\prime}
is not necessarily the closest to
v
, but, crucially for us, enables a low-degree proof for the statement,
“
\Delta(u,v)\geq\Delta(u^{\prime},v)
.”

The above SoS solution can then be "rounded" (via standard techniques) to obtain a vector within the
unique
decoding radius. We augment the SoS algorithm with the unique decoding algorithm for the outer code, to handle the minimization over
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{out}}
, which presented a barrier for the SoS algorithm itself.

1.3
Related work

Our work is closely related to that of Bergamaschi, Golowich, and Gunn
[
BGG24
]
which considered AEL amplification in the context of quantum codes. They give multiple code constructions that are either (capacity-achieving) list decodable quantum codes or QLDPC codes (with optimal rate-distance tradeoffs), but not both simultaneously.

Their results on distance amplification yields QLDPC codes nearing the Singleton bound, that are linear-time unique decodable. However, these codes can only be list decoded with access to a classical side channel, where one can transfer classical bits without errors. To obtain list decodable codes, they give another construction by applying the AEL procedure using (CSS codes obtained from) folded Reed–Solomon codes
[
GR08
]
as outer codes, which are known to have optimal list decoding properties as a function of the rate. However, since folded Reed–Solomon codes are not LDPC, the list decodable quantum codes thus obtained are not QLDPC codes.

Proofs to Algorithms paradigm

Spectral algorithms and semidefinite programming for list decoding have been used since the works of Guruswami and Indyk
[
GI03
]
and Dinur et al.
[
DHK+21
]
. More recently, the “proofs to algorithms” paradigm has been used in the application of the sum-of-squares method to several statistical and combinatorial problems
[
FKP19
]
. In the context of list decoding, this paradigm was used by Richelson and Roy
[
RR23
]
for decoding the
\varepsilon
-balanced code construction of Ta-Shma
[
TS17
]
via an SoS implementation of the distance proof. A similar framework was used by
[
JST23
]
to list decode several classical codes based on spectral expanders, including the AEL distance amplification procedure.

Lower bounds

Another important line of work is on understanding the limitations of SoS-based algorithms. The inability of SoS to reason about vector spaces over finite fields is a property used to
construct lower bounds for the SoS hierarchy, ruling out even subexponential time algorithms
within this hierarchy, for several problems
[
Gri01
,
Sch08
,
Tul09
,
DFHT21
,
HL22
]
. In particular, the recent result of Hopkins and Lin
[
HL22
]
gives an explicit lower bound instance using an expander-based QLDPC code. While their work shows that SoS relaxations cannot distinguish between
\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
for some quantum code
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
, our work can be viewed as proving that this is indeed possible with access to a decoder for the outer code, when
\mathcal{F}
is obtained via the AEL construction.

Discussion and Open Problems.

Our work gives a construction of quantum LDPC codes decodable up to the Johnson bound, and it is an intriguing open problem to construct LDPC codes decodable beyond this threshold, even in the case of classical codes.
One concrete direction arising from the present work is to find a
better reduction
between the decodability of codes constructed by amplification procedures, such as AEL, and the starting points of such constructions.
The current reduction using the SoS hierarchy relies on the expansion used in the amplification procedure and yields decodability up to the Johnson bound. However, it is conceivable that a reduction using additional structure from (a variation of) the amplification procedure can go beyond this threshold.

We also note that while the running times of the algorithms obtained in this work are polynomial
(when
q,d
are taken to be constants), they are very far from the highly efficient linear-time
unique decoding algorithms that are known for classical and quantum LDPC codes. However, we hope
that understanding the structures that facilitate list decoding via SoS can point the way towards
fast spectral algorithms relying on these structures, as has been the case in the past for statistical problems such as tensor decomposition
[
HSSS16
]
, and for decoding some classical codes based on expanders
[
JST21
]
.

Outline

We start by defining the task of list decoding for vector space CSS codes. We also define the notion of duality preserving maps, which we then use in
Section

3
to formalize the central code operations—
code concatenation
and AEL
amplification
— in an explicit linear algebraic way.
Sections

4
and
6
form the key contribution of our work in which we give a new proof of quantum AEL distance amplification. The main idea is to introduce the
partial minimizer
using which we give a distance proof (
Section

4
). After setting up some additional preliminaries in
Section

5
, we show how our distance proof easily generalizes to the SoS setting
Section

6
).
Finally, in
Section

7
, we give a polynomial-time list decoding algorithm by plugging our SoS proof of distance into the machinery of
[
JST23
]
.

2
Preliminaries

Throughout the paper, we will work with
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-vector spaces with the standard basis (unless specified otherwise). The spaces will be equipped with the Hamming metric and the canonical bilinear form
\langle u,v\rangle=\sum_{i}u_{i}v_{i}
, with respect to this basis. We define,
V^{\perp}=\{w\mid\left\langle v,w\right\rangle=0,\,\forall\,v\in V\}
. We will also work with
{\mathbb{R}}
-vector spaces equipped with the expectation inner product and norm.

2.1
Quantum CSS codes and list decoding

Calderbank–Shor, and Steane (independently) showed that a pair of subspaces over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
define a
quantum code if they satisfy a certain orthogonality condition. This construction is known as the CSS construction, and CSS codes form a subclass of
stabilizer codes
.

2.1
CSS Codes.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}\subseteq{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
be
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear subspaces such that
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
. Then,
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
defines a
[[n,k,\delta n]]_{q}
quantum code, where

k=\dim(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})-\dim(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}),\;\;\delta=\frac{1}{n}\min\big\{\left\lvert v\right\rvert\;\big|\;v\in(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp})\cup(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp})\big\}.

The CSS code is low-density parity check (LDPC) if there exist (row and column)-sparse parity check matrices,
H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}
, over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
such that
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}=\ker H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}=\ker H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}
.

Vector space CSS codes

The works
[
BGG24
,
GG23
]
show that the CSS construction can be generalized to the setup where the
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear subspaces have coefficients in a vector space
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b}
. This generalization enables the extension of the classical coding theoretic operation of folding to the quantum setup. We will work with this general definition throughout the paper.

Folding

Folding
is an element-wise syntactic operation in which vectors in
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{bn}
are viewed as vectors in
\left\lparen{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b}\right\rparen^{n}
. Formally, let
V\subseteq{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{bn}
be a vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. For a vector
v\in V
, denote

\fold(v)=(v^{(1)},\cdots,v^{(n)})\in\left\lparen{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b}\right\rparen^{n}\text{ where }v^{(i)}=(v_{(i-1)b+1},\cdots,v_{ib})\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b}.

2.2
Vector Space CSS code.

Let
d
be a positive integer and let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
be a
[[nb,k,\delta nb]]_{q}
CSS code. Then,
\fold(\mathcal{C})
defines a
[[n,k/b,\delta^{\prime}n]]_{q,b}
vector space CSS code wherein,

\delta^{\prime}=\frac{1}{n}\min\big\{\left\lvert\fold(v)\right\rvert\;\big\lvert\;v\in(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp})\cup(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp})\big\}.

The weight,
\left\lvert c\right\rvert
, now is the Hamming weight over the alphabet
q^{b}
, i.e.,
|\fold(v)|=\lvert\{i\mid v^{(i)}\neq 0\}\rvert
.

We will use the notation
[[n,k/b,\delta^{\prime}n]]_{q,b}
to specify that the code consists of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear spaces that have been folded in blocks of size
b
. Unless specified otherwise, the folding will be done sequentially to the coordinates according to the fixed basis. We will use the shortened notation
[[n,k]]
when we do not require to address the distance of the code. We will drop the notation
\fold(\mathcal{C})
when we explicitly mention that a code is a vector space CSS code.

2.3
.

The
“folded dimension”
changes to
k/b
to make it consistent with the classical notion. However, both dimension and blocklength change by the same factor
b
, so that the rate is unchanged. Moreover, the dimensions as
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-subspaces remain unchanged after folding.

List decoding

We now formalize the notion of list decoding for quantum CSS codes and the folded codes. This is inspired by the classical definition but there are a couple of crucial changes in the quantum setup, (i) the input is no longer a corrupted codeword but a
syndrome
, and (ii) the output is required to be a list
containing
the list of possible errors. The first constraint is necessitated by the no-cloning theorem whereas the second relaxation is required as pruning the list can be hard, unlike in the classical case.

2.4
List of codewords.

Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
be a vector space CSS code over
\left\lparen{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d}\right\rparen^{n}
. Let
B(g,\tau)
denote a ball of fractional radius
\tau
, in the Hamming metric over
q^{d}
, around a vector
g
. For any pair of vectors
{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}\in\left\lparen{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d}\right\rparen^{n}
, we define the following lists as lists of cosets of codewords,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{X}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},\tau)~

\displaystyle=~\Big\{{h}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\,\mid\,{h}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\,\;B({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},\tau)\cap{h}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\neq\emptyset\Big\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)~

\displaystyle=~\Big\{{h}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\,\mid\,{h}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\,\;B({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)\cap{h}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\neq\emptyset\Big\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)~

\displaystyle=~{\mathcal{L}}_{X}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},\tau)\,\times\,{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau).

We also define
{\mathcal{L}}_{e}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)
as a list of cosets of errors, which is just the codeword list
{\mathcal{L}}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)
shifted by
{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}
and
{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}
respectively. When decoding from syndromes, all information about the original codeword is lost since the syndrome only depends on the error pattern. Therefore, one can only hope to output a list of errors rather than a list of codewords. In other words, since syndrome is invariant to translation by codewords, so should the output of a list decoding algorithm, and
{\mathcal{L}}_{e}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)
is the translation-invariant version of
{\mathcal{L}}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)
.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{e}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)~

\displaystyle=~\Big\{\big\lparen{h}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}-{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp},{h}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}-{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\big\rparen\,\big|\,\big\lparen{h}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp},{h}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\big\rparen\in{\mathcal{L}}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)\Big\}

\displaystyle=~\left\lparen{\mathcal{L}}_{X}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},\tau)-{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}\right\rparen\times\left\lparen{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)-{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}\right\rparen

\displaystyle=~{\mathcal{L}}_{e}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}^{\prime},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}^{\prime},\tau)\qquad\text{for any}\qquad{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}-{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\,{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}-{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}.

The last equality follows as the codes
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}
are linear and the Hamming metric is translation-invariant.

2.5
List Decodable vector space CSS codes, also in
[
BGG24
]
.

A quantum CSS code
\mathcal{C}
is
(\tau,L)
-list decodable if for every
({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}})
, the list size is bounded, i.e.,
\lvert{\mathcal{L}}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)\rvert\leq L
.

Fix a pair of parity check matrices
(H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
. We say that a code is efficiently list decodable upto fractional radius
\tau
if given
(H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}})
such that
{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}\in B(0,\tau)
, there exists a
{\mathrm{poly}}(n)
-time algorithm that outputs a list that contains
{\mathcal{L}}_{e}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)
.

2.6
.

Assume that for any
({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}})
, one can output the lists
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},\tau)
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)
in
{\mathrm{poly}}(n)
-time. Then, the quantum CSS code is efficiently list decodable.

Proof.

Given,
(H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}})
one use Gaussian elimination to compute
({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}^{\prime},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}^{\prime})
such that
{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}-{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
and
{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}-{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}
. By the assumption we can compute
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}^{\prime},\tau)
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}^{\prime},\tau)
, and therefore output,

{\mathcal{L}}_{e}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)=Bigg\lparen{\mathcal{L}}_{X}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}^{\prime},\tau)-{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}^{\prime}Bigg\rparen\times Bigg\lparen{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}^{\prime},\tau)-{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}^{\prime}Bigg\rparen
.

In summary, we can reduce the task of list decoding the classical codes
\fold(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})
and
\fold(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
but
upto cosets
. Moreover, our decoder will be symmetric and henceforth we will focus on the task of
X
-decoding.

2.7
List decoding vector space codes.

Let
\fold(\mathcal{E})
be a vector space CSS code over
\left\lparen{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d}\right\rparen^{n}
with given parity-check matrices
(H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},H_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
. The task of efficient list decoding
\fold(\mathcal{E})
upto radius
\tau
reduces to the following two tasks,

•

X-decoding
: Given as input
{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}\in\left\lparen{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d}\right\rparen^{n}
, output a list,
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}^{\prime}
of cosets of codewords such that,

{\mathcal{L}}_{X}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},\tau)~\subseteq~{\mathcal{L}}_{X}^{\prime}~\subseteq~\fold(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})/\fold(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}).

•

Z-decoding
: Given as input
{g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}\in\left\lparen{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d}\right\rparen^{n}
, output a list,
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}^{\prime}
of cosets of codewords such that,

{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}({g}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}},\tau)~\subseteq~{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}^{\prime}~\subseteq~\fold(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})/\fold(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}).

2.2
Duality preserving maps

To generalize the notion of concatenation to quantum CSS codes, we will need the notion of duality-preserving maps. These are needed to properly define the concatenated code such that the orthogonality constraint,
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
, is satisfied, thereby defining a quantum CSS code. This has been used in earlier works, for example, see
[
Ham08
]
.

A bilinear map
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle:V\times W\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-vector spaces
V,W
, is
non-degenerate
if for any non-zero
x\in V
, the map
\left\langle x,\cdot\right\rangle:W\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
is not identically zero. Similarly, for a non-zero
y\in W
, the map
\left\langle\cdot,y\right\rangle:V\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
is not the zero map.

2.8
Dual Systems and Basis.

Let
V,W
be
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-vector spaces of equal dimension. Let
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
be a non-degenerate bilinear map
V\times W\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. Then,
(V,W,\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle)
defines a dual system. A basis
\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{b}\}
of
V
, and
\{w_{1},\cdots,w_{b}\}
of
W
is said to be dual if
\left\langle v_{i},w_{j}\right\rangle=\delta_{ij}
for all
i,j\in[b]
.

The spaces
V,W
are called
dual spaces
as the bilinear map gives an isomorphism
W\rightarrow V^{*}
defined as
w\mapsto\left\langle w,\cdot\right\rangle
, which also proves the existence of such basis. This map is injective as the bilinear map is non-degenerate. Now, one can use the canonical dual basis of
V^{*}
. Using a dual basis, one can construct
duality-preserving maps
which are what we will need.

2.9
Duality preserving map.

Let
(V_{1},V_{2},\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{V})
and
(W_{1},W_{2},\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{W})
be two dual systems. A pair of linear maps
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
where
\varphi_{i}:V_{i}\rightarrow W_{i}
are duality preserving if,

\left\langle u,v\right\rangle_{V}=\left\langle\varphi_{1}(u),\varphi_{2}(v)\right\rangle_{W}\;\;\forall\,u,v\in V.

(1)

2.10
.

Let
(V_{1},V_{2})
and be
(W_{1},W_{2})
dual systems along with their pairs of dual bases. Then,
\varphi_{i}:V_{i}\mapsto W_{i}
that acts as identity with respect to these dual bases is a duality-preserving map.

Proof.

Since the condition is bilinear, it suffices to prove it for any pair of basis vectors of
V_{1},V_{2}
.

\left\langle u_{i},v_{j}\right\rangle_{V}=\delta_{ij}=\left\langle x_{i},y_{j}\right\rangle_{V}=\left\langle\varphi_{1}(u_{i}),\varphi_{2}(y_{j})\right\rangle_{W}.\qquad\hbox{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}

We now define two different dual systems we work with. Both use the canonical bilinear form over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
, albeit for different
n
. The first is the space
({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b},{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b})
which forms a dual system with the elementary basis as a dual basis. The second system will be subspaces of a CSS code. We now prove that this second system forms a dual system.

2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
be a CSS code over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d}
of dimension
k
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}={W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}={W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
respectively. Then, the canonical bilinear form over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
is non-degenerate over
{W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}\,\times\,{W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}
.
Therefore, there exists a pair of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
–linear isomorphisms
{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}:{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b}\rightarrow W_{X}
,
{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}:{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b}\rightarrow W_{Z}
such that,

\left\langle x,y\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b}}=\left\langle{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(x),{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}(y)\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{d}}\;\;\forall\,x,y\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}\,.

Proof.

The space
({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k},{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k})
with the canonical bilinear form is a dual system with the elementary basis as a dual basis. Therefore, if
({W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}})
equipped with the canonical bilinear form is a dual system, then
2.10
yields a duality-preserving isomorphism as needed. To show this, we only need to prove the non-degeneracy of the canonical form, and we will do so for one component as the argument is symmetric.

Let
v\in{W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}
be such that
\left\langle u,v\right\rangle=0
for all
u\in{W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}
. Since,
v\in{W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}
, we have
\left\langle w,v\right\rangle=0
for all
w\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
. But,
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}={W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
and therefore,
v\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
. Since,
{W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}\cap\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}=\{0\}
,
v
must be
0
.

Changing base field

We now see that one can view a CSS code over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
as a vector space CSS code over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b}
. To do this we first
equip
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
with the
trace form
.

2.12
Trace Map.

Let
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
be a degree
k
-extension of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. The trace map
1
1

1

We will drop the subscript as we will not work with multiple extensions.
is defined as

\mathsf{Tr}_{\,{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}:{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q},\;\;x\mapsto x+x^{q}+x^{q^{2}}+\cdots+x^{q^{b}}.

The trace map is
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear as for any
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
,
a^{q}=a
.

It is a well-known fact that the trace map defines a non-degenerate
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-bilinear map over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
defined as
\left\langle x,y\right\rangle_{\mathsf{Tr}}=\mathsf{Tr}(xy)
. Therefore,
({\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}},{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}},\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\mathsf{Tr}})
forms a dual system, and so from
2.10
, there exists a pair of maps
{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},\varphi_{z}:{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b}
, that is duality preserving. We denote by
(\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}},\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}})
, the map obtained on
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}^{n}
obtained by applying
({\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}})
on each coordinate.

2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
[[n,k,\delta n]]_{q^{b}}
CSS code over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
, and let
({\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}})
be the duality preserving map defined above. Then
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\lparen\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}),\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})\rparen
is a
[[n,k,\delta n]]_{q,b}
vector space CSS code, where

Proof.

To see that it is a CSS code, let
u=\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}}(u^{\prime})\in\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})^{\perp}
. The vector
u
can be written this way as
\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}}
is an isomorphism. Then, for any
v\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}
,

\displaystyle\left\langle u^{\prime},v\right\rangle=\left\langle\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}}(u^{\prime}),\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}}(v)\right\rangle=0.

Thus,
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
and therefore,
\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})^{\perp}\subseteq\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp})\subseteq\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}}(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})
which is the CSS condition.

Since
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
is a
{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}
- subspace,
\dim({\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}))
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
is
b\cdot\dim(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})
. Thus, the dimension of the new (unfolded code) is
\dim({\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}))-\dim({\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})^{\perp})=b\cdot k
which becomes
k
after folding.
The distance is unchanged as for
v=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q^{b}}^{n}
the inital weight is,

\lvert v\rvert=\lvert\{i\mid v_{i}\neq 0\}\rvert==\lvert\{i\mid{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(v_{i})\neq 0\}\rvert=\lvert{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(v)\rvert.

2.3
Expander Graphs

For a bipartite graph
G=(L,R,E)
, let
L
be the set of left vertices, and
R
be the set of right vertices. Let
A_{G}
denote the
L\times R
biadjacency matrix, and
\sigma_{2}(A_{G})
be its second largest singular value.

2.14
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander.

A
d
-regular bipartite graph
G(L,R,E)
with
|L|=|R|=n
is said to be an
(n,d,\lambda)
–expander if
\sigma_{2}(A_{G})~\leq~\lambda\cdot d
.

Infinite families of
(n,d,\lambda)
–expanders, with growing
n
as
d
and
\lambda
are constant, can be derived based on double covers of Ramanujan graphs of
[
LPS88
]
as long as
\lambda\geq\frac{2\sqrt{d-1}}{d}
.

2.15
Expander Mixing Lemma.

Given an
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander
G=(L,R,E)
and functions
f:L\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
and
g:R\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, the following well–known property is a simple consequence of definition of
(n,d,\lambda)
–expanders:

\Big\lvert\mathchoice{\underset{(\ell,r)\sim E}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f(\ell)\cdot g(r)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{(\ell,r)\sim E}[f(\ell)\cdot g(r)]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{(\ell,r)\sim E}[f(\ell)\cdot g(r)]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{(\ell,r)\sim E}[f(\ell)\cdot g(r)]}-\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\sim L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f(\ell)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim L}[f(\ell)]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim L}[f(\ell)]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim L}[f(\ell)]}\cdot\mathchoice{\underset{r\sim R}{\mathbb{E}}\left[g(r)\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\sim R}[g(r)]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\sim R}[g(r)]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\sim R}[g(r)]}\Big\rvert\leq\lambda\cdot\lVert f\rVert_{2}\lVert g\rVert_{2}\,.

3
Concatenated Codes and AEL Amplification

In this section, we formalize the quantum CSS generalizations of two operations on classical codes –
code concatenation
and AEL
distance amplification
(
[
AEL95
]
). We define it purely in linear algebraic terms but the recent work
[
BGG24
]
also formalizes this using the stabilizer code framework.

3.1
Concatenation of CSS Codes

To define a concatenation of CSS codes, one needs a pair of outer code and inner code that are compatible with respect to some parameters. Additionally in the quantum case, we need a pair of duality-preserving maps as defined in
Lemma

2.11
.

3.1
Concatenated CSS Codes.

Given the following objects,

•

Outer Code
— Let
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
be a
[[n,k_{\mathrm{out}},\delta_{\mathrm{out}}]]_{q,b_{\mathrm{out}}}
vector space CSS code.

•

Inner Code
— Let
\mathcal{C}=(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
be a
[[d,k_{\mathrm{in}},\delta_{\mathrm{in}}]]_{q,b_{\mathrm{in}}}
vector space CSS code that
b_{\mathrm{in}}k_{\mathrm{in}}=b_{\mathrm{out}}
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}=W_{X}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}=W_{Z}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
respectively (as
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{db_{\mathrm{in}}}
-subspaces).

•

Duality preserving maps
— Let
({\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}},{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}})
be duality preserving maps as in
Lemma

2.11
from
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}
to
{W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}
and
{W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}
respectively. Extend these maps to
({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k})^{n}
by applying it to each coordinate of the folded code, and call the extended map
(\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}},\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}})
.

One defines the concatenated CSS Code,
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{D}\circ_{\varphi}\mathcal{C}
as
(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}}(\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})+\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\subseteq{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{ndb_{\mathrm{in}}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}}(\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})+\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\subseteq{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{ndb_{\mathrm{in}}}

We now give an explicit description of the dual spaces that will prove that the concatenation operation defines a CSS code and also be useful in proving the distance of the final code.

3.2
.

For the above definition of
(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
, the dual spaces can be computed as follows,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}}(\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp})+\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}}(\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp})+\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}

Therefore,
\mathcal{E}
is an
[[nd,{k_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot k_{\mathrm{in}}}]]_{q,b_{\mathrm{in}}}
vector space CSS code.
Moreover, if
\mathcal{D}
is an LDPC code, and
db_{\mathrm{in}}
is a constant, then
\mathcal{E}
is an LDPC code.

Proof.

We prove the first equation as the proofs are symmetric. Define,

\mathcal{U}_{X}:=\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}}(\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp})+\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}.

Since
\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}}
is an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear isomorphism
\dim(\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}}(U))=\dim(U)
. Moreover,
\mathrm{im}(\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}})
and
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
are disjoint and thus their dimension add up. Thus,

\displaystyle\dim(\mathcal{U}_{X})~

\displaystyle=~\dim(\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp})+n\,\dim(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp})=nb_{\mathrm{out}}-\dim(\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})+n(db_{\mathrm{in}}-\dim(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})),

\displaystyle\dim(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})~

\displaystyle=~\dim(\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})+n(\dim(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}))=\dim(\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})+n(\dim(\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})-k_{\mathrm{in}}).

Therefore,
\dim(\mathcal{U}_{X})=ndb_{\mathrm{in}}-\dim(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})=\dim(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp})
and it suffices to show that
\,\mathcal{U}_{X}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathcal{U}_{X}
and
\beta\in\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
. Using the definition of the spaces, we express them as,

\displaystyle\alpha~

\displaystyle=~({\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}(u^{(1)})+x_{1},\,\cdots\,,{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}(u^{(n)})+x_{n})\in\mathcal{U}_{X}\;\text{ where }x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp},\,u=(u^{(1)},\cdots,u^{(n)})\in\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp},

\displaystyle\beta~

\displaystyle=~({\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(v^{(n)})+z_{1},\,\cdots\,,{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(v^{(n)})+z_{n})\in\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}\;\;\,\text{ where }z_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp},\,v=(v^{(1)},\cdots,v^{(n)})\in\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}.

Computing the inner product we get four kinds of terms,

\left\langle\alpha,\beta\right\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\lparen\left\langle{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}\big\lparen u^{(i)}\big\rparen,{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}\big\lparen v^{(i)}\big\rparen\right\rangle+\left\langle{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}\big\lparen u^{(i)}\big\rparen,z_{i}\right\rangle+\left\langle x_{i},{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}\big\lparen v^{(i)}\big\rparen\right\rangle+\left\langle x_{i},z_{i}\right\rangle\Big\rparen

Each of the last three terms is zero as, by definition, they belong to orthogonal spaces.
We are then left with the first term which can be calculated using the duality-preserving property,

\displaystyle\left\langle\alpha,\beta\right\rangle~

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\langle{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}\big\lparen u^{(i)}\big\rparen\;,\;{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}\big\lparen v^{(i)}\big\rparen\Big\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{db_{\mathrm{in}}}}

\displaystyle=~\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\langle u^{(i)},v^{(i)}\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b_{\mathrm{out}}}}

(Duality Preserving)

\displaystyle=~\left\langle u,v\right\rangle_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{nb_{\mathrm{out}}}}=0

\displaystyle(u\in\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp},v\in\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}).

This proves that
\mathcal{U}_{X}=\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}=V_{X}\oplus\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
, the proof implies that
\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}=\varphi_{X}(V_{X})\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
. Recall that for a
[[n,k_{\mathrm{out}}]]_{q,b_{\mathrm{out}}}
vector space code, the dimension of
V_{X}
as a
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-subspace is
k_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot b_{\mathrm{out}}
.
Thus, the dimension of the CSS code
\mathcal{E}
is,

\dim(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})-\dim(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp})=\dim(\varphi_{X}(V_{X}))=\dim(V_{X})=k_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot b_{\mathrm{out}}=k_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot k_{\mathrm{in}}b_{\mathrm{in}}\quad.

Folding this
\mathcal{E}
into blocks of size
b_{\mathrm{in}}
, we get a
[[nd,k_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot k_{\mathrm{in}}]]_{q,b_{\mathrm{in}}}
vector space CSS code.
The LDPC property follows since the generators of
\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
are comprised of generators of
\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
mapped by
\widetilde{{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}}
, and generators corresponding to
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
. The former are sparse if
\mathcal{D}
is LDPC, and the latter has weight at most
db_{\mathrm{in}}
which is a constant.

3.2
AEL Amplification and Folding CSS Codes

The distance of the concatenated code
\mathcal{E}
can be amplified by the AEL procedure using a
d
-regular bipartite expander,
G=(L,R,E)
.
The graph
G
is chosen such that the size of
L
and
R
match the blocklength of the outer code, and the degree matches the blocklength of the inner code.

The AEL procedure is a three-step process — (i) concatenate the outer code
\mathcal{D}
with inner code
\mathcal{C}
to obtain
\mathcal{E}
, (ii) shuffle the symbols of concatenated code via edges on a bipartite expander graph
G
, and (iii) collect
d
-symbols on the right vertices and fold them back to produce the final code,
\mathcal{F}
.

In this subsection, we will formally state the AEL procedure and set up some useful notation. We start by restating the definitions of the concatenated codes (and their duals) in a manner that will be convenient when working with AEL, and later, with sum-of-squares proofs.

Concatenated Codes and AEL

To simplify notation, will use
\Sigma
to denote
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b_{\mathrm{in}}}
as the concatenated code lies inside the space
({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b_{\mathrm{in}}})^{nd}=\Sigma^{E}
. We view the codewords,
z\in\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
(or
\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}
), as an assignment of
\Sigma
-values to the edges. Denote by
z_{\ell}\in\Sigma^{d}
, the restriction of the vector
z
to the neighborhood,
N(\ell)
, of vertex
\ell\in L
. We will use similarly use
z_{r}
, to denote restriction to the neighborhood of vertex
r\in R
.
The concatenated code
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{D}\circ_{\varphi}\mathcal{C}
and its duals can be explicitly described as,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}~

\displaystyle=~\left\{x\mid x_{\ell}={\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(u_{\ell})+z_{\ell},\text{ for a unique }z_{\ell}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\text{ and }u\in\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}~

\displaystyle=~\left\{x\mid x_{\ell}={\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(u_{\ell})+z_{\ell},\text{ for a unique }z_{\ell}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\text{ and }u\in\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}~

\displaystyle=~\left\{x\mid x_{\ell}={\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(u_{\ell})+z_{\ell},\text{ for a unique }z_{\ell}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\text{ and }v\in\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}~

\displaystyle=~\left\{x\mid x_{\ell}={\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(u_{\ell})+z_{\ell},\text{ for a unique }z_{\ell}\in\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\text{ and }u\in\mathcal{D}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}\right\}.

Uniqueness follows as
\mathrm{im}({\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}})={W}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}
is disjoint from
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
(and similarly for
{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}
). The concatenated code
\mathcal{E}
is folded using the partitions induced by the neighborhoods of the right vertices. Explicitly, the folded code
\mathcal{F}=\fold(\mathcal{E})=(\fold(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}),\fold(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}))
is given by,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}:=~\fold(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}})~

\displaystyle=~\Big\{\fold(z_{r_{1}},\cdots,z_{r_{n}})\big|\,z\in\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\;r_{i}\in R\Big\}\subseteq\left\lparen\Sigma^{d}\right\rparen^{n}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}:=~\fold(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})~

\displaystyle=~\Big\{\fold(z_{r_{1}},\cdots,z_{r_{n}})\big|\,z\in\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}},\;r_{i}\in R\Big\}\subseteq\left\lparen\Sigma^{d}\right\rparen^{n}

3.3
AEL Procedure.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
[[n,k_{\mathrm{out}}]]_{q,b_{\mathrm{out}}}
CSS code and
\mathcal{C}
be a
[[d,k_{\mathrm{in}}]]_{q,b_{\mathrm{in}}}
CSS code.

Then, the AEL code,
\mathcal{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}})
defines a
[[n,\frac{k_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot k_{\mathrm{in}}}{d},\delta_{R}\cdot n]]_{q,db_{\mathrm{in}}}
CSS code where

\delta_{R}\cdot n=\min\big\{\,\big\lvert\{i\mid z_{r_{i}}\neq 0\}\big\rvert\;\big|\;z\in(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp})\cup(\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}^{\perp})\big\}.

The key property of the amplified code,
\mathcal{F}
, is that its distance is significantly better than
\mathcal{E}
. We will prove a lower bound on
\delta_{R}
in the next section (
Theorem

4.2
). Before that, we define two notions that we use later:
local inversion maps
, and a couple different distance metrics over
\Sigma^{dn}
.

Local Inversion

The uniqueness of decomposition of the local codeword,
x_{\ell}
, let us define an inverse to the maps
{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}
and
{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}
.

3.4
Local Inversion Maps.

Let
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{D}\circ_{\varphi}\mathcal{C}
be the concatenated code as above. Then one defines local inversion maps,

\displaystyle{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}^{\raisebox{0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}:\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b_{\mathrm{out}}},\;\;x_{\ell}~

\displaystyle=~{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{X}\mathstrut}}(u_{\ell})+z_{\ell}\mapsto u_{\ell},

\displaystyle{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}^{\raisebox{0.90417pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}:\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{b_{\mathrm{out}}},\;\;x_{\ell}~

\displaystyle=~\,{\varphi}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{{Z}\mathstrut}}(v_{\ell})+z_{\ell}\mapsto v_{\ell}.

Distance metrics for
\mathcal{E}

Using the graph structure, we can fold the code
\mathcal{E}
using the left or right vertices. Moreover, we can define a define a metric for the set of
\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
cosets which is needed for the quantum notion of distance.

\displaystyle\Delta_{L}(z,h)~

\displaystyle=~\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\sim L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}]},

\displaystyle\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(z,h)~

\displaystyle=~\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\sim L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\sim L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}]},

\displaystyle\Delta_{R}(z,h)~

\displaystyle=~\mathchoice{\underset{r\sim R}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\sim R}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\sim R}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{r\sim R}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{z_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}]}.

Now we can reinterpret AEL procedure as changing the metric from the initial
\Delta_{L}(\cdot,\cdot)
on the concatenated code
\mathcal{E}
to
\Delta_{R}(\cdot,\cdot)
. This change is crucial as this is where the pseudorandom properties of the graph (expansion) come in, and imply that the distance between codewords under the
\Delta_{R}
metric is much larger than the initial distance under the
\Delta_{L}
metric.

Decoders for AEL

As defined in
2.7
, a list decoder up to radius
\tau
for the code
\mathcal{F}
should take as input a string
g\in(\Sigma^{d})^{R}
, and output a list of cosets of
\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
containing

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(g,\tau)~

\displaystyle=~\big\{\fold(h)+\mathcal{F}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}{\;\;:\;\;}h\in\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\,\Delta_{R}(g,h)<\tau\big\}

\displaystyle\cong~\big\{h+\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}{\;\;:\;\;}h\in\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}},\,\Delta_{R}(g,h)<\tau\big\}.

The second set is merely the unfolded version of the first and it is equivalent to work with either. We will work with the latter to simplify notation.

4
Distance Proofs

In this section, we prove that the folded AEL code,
\fold(\mathcal{D}\circ_{\varphi}\mathcal{C})
has large (fractional) distance,
\delta_{R}
, that can be made arbitrarily close to that of the inner code
\delta_{\mathrm{in}}
by picking a good enough expander.

4.1
Partial Minimizer

As mentioned before, one of the key objects we will use in the distance proof is a partial minimizer, which does not change the coset, but gets closer to the codeword we are measuring distance from. For a fixed codeword
h\in\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
, and any vector
z\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{E}
we define the partial minimizer to be a new vector
\psi(z,h)

\psi(z,h)_{\ell}=\begin{cases}z_{\ell}\;\;\text{ if }\;\;z_{\ell}\not\in\,h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\\[6.0pt]
h_{\ell}\;\;\text{ if }\;\;z_{\ell}\in\,h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\end{cases}

We observe that the partial minimizer satisfies two key properties,

(
Coset-preserving)

\displaystyle\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\in\,z_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\;\;\forall\,\ell\in L

(2)

(
Monotone)

\displaystyle\Delta(\psi(z,h)_{r},h_{r})\leq\Delta(z_{r},h_{r})\;\;\forall\,r\in\,R

(3)

4.2
Distance proof

We next prove for any two codewords in
\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
that do not share the same coset of
\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
, their distance in the
\Delta_{R}(\cdot,\cdot)
metric is almost as large as
\delta_{\mathrm{in}}
.

4.1
Distance proof of AEL.

Let
z
and
h
be two non-equivalent codewords in
\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{X\mathstrut}}
, i.e.,
z\not\in h+\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
. Then,

\Delta_{R}(z,h)\geq\delta:=\delta_{\mathrm{in}}-\frac{\lambda}{\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(z,h)}

where
\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(z,h)=\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathbf{1}}\big\{z_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\big\}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{\mathbf{1}}\big\{z_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\big\}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{\mathbf{1}}\big\{z_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\big\}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{\mathbf{1}}\big\{z_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\big\}]}
.

Proof.

We will lower bound and upper bound the same quantity
\Delta_{E}(\psi(z,h),h)
. Note that by
Eq.

2
, we have
\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(z,h)=\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(\psi(z,h),h)
.

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\underset{e\in E}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\psi(z,h)_{e}\neq h_{e}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in E}[\psi(z,h)_{e}\neq h_{e}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in E}[\psi(z,h)_{e}\neq h_{e}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in E}[\psi(z,h)_{e}\neq h_{e}]}~

\displaystyle=~\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\Delta\left\lparen\psi(z,h)_{\ell},h_{\ell}\right\rparen}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{\Delta\left\lparen\psi(z,h)_{\ell},h_{\ell}\right\rparen}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{\Delta\left\lparen\psi(z,h)_{\ell},h_{\ell}\right\rparen}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{\Delta\left\lparen\psi(z,h)_{\ell},h_{\ell}\right\rparen}]}

\displaystyle\geq~\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}\cdot\Delta\left\lparen\psi(z,h)_{\ell},h_{\ell}\right\rparen\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}\cdot\Delta\left\lparen\psi(z,h)_{\ell},h_{\ell}\right\rparen]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}\cdot\Delta\left\lparen\psi(z,h)_{\ell},h_{\ell}\right\rparen]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}\cdot\Delta\left\lparen\psi(z,h)_{\ell},h_{\ell}\right\rparen]}

\displaystyle\geq~\mathchoice{\underset{\ell\in L}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}\cdot\delta_{\mathrm{in}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}\cdot\delta_{\mathrm{in}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}\cdot\delta_{\mathrm{in}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell\in L}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}\right\}}\cdot\delta_{\mathrm{in}}]}

\displaystyle=~\delta_{\mathrm{in}}\cdot\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(\psi(z,h),h)

\displaystyle=~\delta_{\mathrm{in}}\cdot\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(z,h)

\displaystyle(\text{Using \lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{eqn:coset}}).

For the upper bound, we will use the expander mixing lemma (
Lemma

2.15
).

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\underset{e\in E}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{e}\neq h_{e}\right\}}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in E}[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{e}\neq h_{e}\right\}}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in E}[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{e}\neq h_{e}\right\}}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{e\in E}[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{e}\neq h_{e}\right\}}}]}~

\displaystyle\leq~\mathchoice{\underset{(\ell,r)\,\in\,E}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}\cdot{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{(\ell,r)\,\in\,E}[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}\cdot{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{(\ell,r)\,\in\,E}[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}\cdot{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{(\ell,r)\,\in\,E}[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}\cdot{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}}]}

\displaystyle\leq~\mathchoice{\underset{\ell,r}{\mathbb{E}}\left[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}\cdot{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}}\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell,r}[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}\cdot{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell,r}[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}\cdot{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}}]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell,r}[{{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{\ell}\neq h_{\ell}\right\}}\cdot{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\psi(z,h)_{r}\neq h_{r}\right\}}}]}+\lambda

(
Lemma

2.15
)

\displaystyle=~\Delta_{L}(\psi(z,h),h)\cdot\Delta_{R}(\psi(z,h),h)+\lambda

\displaystyle=~\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(\psi(z,h),h)\cdot\Delta_{R}(\psi(z,h),h)+\lambda

(Definition of
\psi
)

\displaystyle=~\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(z,h)\cdot\Delta_{R}(\psi(z,h),h)+\lambda

(
Eq.

2
)

\displaystyle\leq~\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(z,h)\cdot\Delta_{R}(z,h)+\lambda

(
Eq.

3
)

Comparing the two sides, we get

\displaystyle\Delta_{R}(z,h)\cdot\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(z,h)+\lambda~\geq~\delta_{\mathrm{in}}\cdot\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(z,h)

Since
z\not\in h+\mathcal{E}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
, there exists at least one vertex
\ell\in L
such that
z_{\ell}\not\in h_{\ell}+\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}
and thus,
\Delta_{L,\mathcal{C}_{\lx@scalerel@obj{Z\mathstrut}}^{\perp}}(z,h)>0
. Dividing by it gives the result.

We now deduce that using AEL machinery amplifies the distance of the base outer code.

4.2
AEL distance.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a
[[n,k_{\mathrm{out}},\delta_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot n]]_{q,b_{\mathrm{out}}}
vector space CSS code and
\mathcal{C}
be a
[[d,k_{\mathrm{in}},\delta_{\mathrm{in}}\cdot d]]_{q,b_{\mathrm{in}}}
vector space CSS code.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be the AEL code obtained by using an
(n,d,\lambda)
-expander. Then,
\mathcal{F}
is a
[[n,\frac{k_{\mathrm{out}}\cdot k_{\mathrm{in}}}{d},\delta_{R}\cdot n]]_{q,db_{\mathrm{in}}}
vector space CSS code where
\delta_{R}\geq\delta_{\mathrm{in}}-\frac{\lambda}{\delta_{\mathrm{out}}}
.

Proof.

Proposition

3.3
gives the dimension of the code. To compute the distance, we observe that
\delta_{R}=\min_{v}\Delta_{R}\left\lparen v\,,0\right\rparen
where the min is over non-trivial codewords. The result now follows from the above distance bound
Lemma

4.1
.

As mentioned before, we will need an SoS version of the above distance proof for our decoding algorithm.
Before describing this SoS distance proof and how the above defined notion of partial minimizer assists it, we will need some additional preliminaries about the Sum-of-Squares hierarchy. We discuss these preliminaries in
Section

5
, and return to the distance proof in
Section

6
.

5
Additional Preliminaries: Sum-of-Squares Hierarchy

The sum-of-squares hierarchy of semidefinite programs (SDPs) provides a family of increasingly
powerful convex relaxations for several optimization problems.
Each “level"
t
of the hierarchy is parametrized by a set of constraints corresponding to
polynomials of degree at most
t
in the optimization variables. While the relaxations in the
hierarchy can be viewed as semidefinite programs of size
n^{O(t)}

[
BS14
,
FKP19
]
,
it is often convenient to view the solution as a linear operator, called the “pseudoexpectation" operator.

Pseudoexpectations

Let
t
be a positive even integer and fix an alphabet
\Sigma
of size
s
. Let
\mathbf{Z}=\{Z_{i,j}\}_{i\in[m],j\in[s]}
be a collection of variables and
{\mathbb{R}}[\mathbf{Z}]^{\leq t}
be the vector space of polynomials of degree at most
t
in the variables
\mathbf{Z}
(including the constants).

5.1
Constrained Pseudoexpectations.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}=\left\{f_{1}=0,\ldots,f_{m}=0,g_{1}\geq 0,\ldots,g_{r}\geq 0\right\}
be a system of
polynomial constraints in
\mathbf{Z}
, with each polynomial in
{\mathcal{S}}
of degree at most
t
. We say
{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[\cdot]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[\cdot]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[\cdot]}}
is a pseudoexpectation operator of SoS-degree
t
, over the variables
\mathbf{Z}
respecting
{\mathcal{S}}
, if it is a linear operator
{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[\cdot]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[\cdot]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[\cdot]}}:{\mathbb{R}}[\mathbf{Z}]^{\leq t}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that:

1.

{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[1\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[1]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[1]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[1]}}=1
.

2.

{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p^{2}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p^{2}]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p^{2}]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p^{2}]}}\geq 0
if
p
is a polynomial in
\mathbf{Z}=\{Z_{i,j}\}_{i\in[m],j\in[s]}
of degree
\leq t/2
.

3.

{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p\cdot f_{i}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p\cdot f_{i}]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p\cdot f_{i}]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p\cdot f_{i}]}}=0
,
\forall\,i\in[m]
and
\forall\,p
such that
\deg(p\cdot f_{i})\leq t
.

4.

{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p^{2}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}g_{i}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p^{2}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}g_{i}]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p^{2}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}g_{i}]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p^{2}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}g_{i}]}}\geq 0
,
\forall\,S\subseteq[r]
and
\forall\,p
such that
\deg(p^{2}\cdot\prod_{i\in S}g_{i})\leq t
.

Let
\mu
be a distribution over the set of assignments,
\Sigma^{m}
. Define the following collection of random variables,

\mathbf{Z}=\big\{\,Z_{i,j}={{\mathbf{1}}\left\{i\mapsto j\right\}}\,\mid\,i\in[m],\,j\in[s]\big\}.

Then, setting
{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[p(\mathbf{Z})\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p(\mathbf{Z})]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p(\mathbf{Z})]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[p(\mathbf{Z})]}}=\mathchoice{\underset{\mu}{\mathbb{E}}\left[p(\mathbf{Z})\right]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[p(\mathbf{Z})]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[p(\mathbf{Z})]}{{\mathbb{E}}_{\mu}[p(\mathbf{Z})]}
for any polynomial
p(\cdot)
defines an (unconstrained) pseudoexpectation operator. However, the reverse is not true when
t<m
, and there can be degree-
t
pseudoexpectations that do not correspond to any genuine distribution,
\mu
. Therefore, the set of all pseudoexpectations should be seen as a relaxation for the set of all possible distributions over such assignments. The upshot of this relaxation is that it is possible to optimize over the set. Under certain conditions on the bit-complexity of solutions
[
O’D17
,
RW17
]
, one can optimize over the set of degree-
t
pseudoexpectations in time
m^{O(t)}
via SDPs.

Local constraints and local functions.

Any constraint that involves at most
k
variables from
\mathbf{Z}
, with
k\leq t
, can be written as a degree-
k
polynomial, and such constraints may be enforced into the SoS solution.
In particular, we will always consider the following canonical constraints on the variables
\mathbf{Z}
.

Z_{i,j}^{2}=Z_{i,j},\ \forall\,i\in[m],j\in[s]\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j}Z_{i,j}=1,\ \forall\,i\in[m]\,.

We will also consider additional constraints and corresponding polynomials, defined by “local" functions. For any
f\in\Sigma^{m}
and
M\subseteq[m]
, we use
f_{M}
to denote the restriction
f|_{M}
, and
f_{i}
to denote
f_{\{i\}}
for convenience.

5.2
k
-local function.

A function
\mu:\Sigma^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is called
k
-local if there is a set
M\subseteq[m]
of size
k
such that
\mu(f)
only depends on
\left\{f(i)\right\}_{i\in M}
, or equivalently,
\mu(f)
only depends on
f|_{M}
.

If
\mu
is
k
-local, we abuse notation to also use
\mu:\Sigma^{M}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
with
\mu(\alpha)=\mu(f)
for any
f
such that
f|_{M}=\alpha
. It will be clear from the input to the function
\mu
whether we are using
\mu
as a function on
\Sigma^{m}
or
\Sigma^{M}
.

Let
\mu:\Sigma^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a
k
-local function that depends on coordinates
M\subseteq[m]
with
|M|=k
. Then
\mu
can be written as a degree-
k
polynomial
p_{\mu}
in
\mathbf{Z}
:

p_{\mu}(\mathbf{Z})=\sum_{\alpha\in\Sigma^{M}}\Big\lparen\mu(\alpha)\cdot\prod_{i\in M}Z_{i,\alpha_{i}}\Big\rparen

With some abuse of notation, we let
\mu(\mathbf{Z})
denote
p_{\mu}(\mathbf{Z})
. We will use such
k
-local
functions inside
{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[\cdot\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[\cdot]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[\cdot]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[\cdot]}}
freely without worrying about their polynomial
representation. For example,
{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\mathbf{Z}_{i}\neq j\right\}}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\mathbf{Z}_{i}\neq j\right\}}]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\mathbf{Z}_{i}\neq j\right\}}]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\mathbf{Z}_{i}\neq j\right\}}]}}
denotes
{\mathchoice{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}\left[1-Z_{i,j}\right]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[1-Z_{i,j}]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[1-Z_{i,j}]}{{\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}}[1-Z_{i,j}]}}
.
The notion of
k
-local functions can also be extended from real valued functions to vector valued functions in a straightforward way.

5.3
Vector-valued Local functions.

A function
\mu:\Sigma^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
is
k
-local if the
N
real valued functions corresponding to the
N
coordinates are all
k
-local. Note that these different coordinate functions may depend on different sets of variables, as long as the number is at most
k
for each of the functions.

Local distribution view of SoS

It will be convenient to use a shorthand for the function
{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\mathbf{Z}_{A}=\alpha\right\}}
, and we will use
\mathbf{Z}_{A,\alpha}
. Likewise, we use
\mathbf{Z}_{i,j}
as a shorthand for the function
{{\mathbf{1}}\left\{\mathbf{Z}_{i}=j\right\}}
. That is, henceforth,
